Quasitriangular coideal subalgebras of U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) in terms of generalized Satake diagrams
Vidas Regelskis, Bart Vlaar

Abstract
. Let 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional semisimple complex Lie algebra and θ \theta an involutive automorphism of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . According to G. Letzter, S. Kolb and M. Balagović the fixed-point subalgebra 𝔨 = 𝔤 θ \mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta} has a quantum counterpart B B , a coideal subalgebra of the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) possessing a universal K-matrix 𝒦 \mathcal{K} . The objects θ \theta , 𝔨 \mathfrak{k} , B B and 𝒦 \mathcal{K} can all be described in terms of Satake diagrams. In the present work we extend this construction to generalized Satake diagrams, combinatorial data first considered by A. Heck. A generalized Satake diagram naturally defines a semisimple automorphism θ \theta of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} restricting to the standard Cartan subalgebra 𝔥 \mathfrak{h} as an involution. It also defines a subalgebra 𝔨 ⊂ 𝔤 \mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g} satisfying 𝔨 ∩ 𝔥 = 𝔥 θ \mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}^{\theta} , but not necessarily a fixed-point subalgebra. The subalgebra 𝔨 \mathfrak{k} can be quantized to a coideal subalgebra of U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) endowed with a universal K-matrix in the sense of Kolb and Balagović. We conjecture that all such coideal subalgebras of U q ​ ( 𝔤 ) U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}) arise from generalized Satake diagrams in this way.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B37, 17B05; Secondary 81R50

1.
Introduction

Given a finite-dimensional semisimple complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and an involutive Lie algebra automorphism
\theta\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
, a
symmetric pair
is a pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
where
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
is the corresponding fixed-point subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, see
[
Ar62
,
Sa71
]
.

Quantum symmetric pairs
are their quantum analogons.
That is, the enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
can be quantized to a quasitriangular Hopf algebra, the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
endowed with the universal R-matrix
\mathcal{R}
, see
[
Ji85
,
Dr87
]
.
The quantum analogon of
\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
is a coideal subalgebra
B\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})

[
Le99
,
Le02
,
Ko14
]
having a compatible quasitriangular structure, the universal K-matrix
\mathcal{K}

[
BK19
,
Ko20
]
(see also
[
BW18a
, Sec. 2.5]
for the case of quantum symmetric pairs of type AIII/AIV).
Quantizations of symmetric pairs appeared earlier in a different approach in
[
NDS95
,
NS95
]
(also see
[
KS09
]
).
A prior notion of a universal K-matrix, not directly linked to a quantum symmetric pair, appeared in
[
DKM03
]
.

The map
\theta
, the fixed-point subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
, the coideal subalgebra
B
and the universal object
\mathcal{K}
are all defined in terms of combinatorial information, the so-called Satake diagram
(X,\tau)
.
Here
X
is a subdiagram of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\tau
is an involutive diagram automorphism stabilizing
X
and satisfying certain compatibility conditions, see
[
Le02
,
Ko14
]
.

It is the aim of this paper to extend some of this work to a more general setting.
A direct motivation for this is the fact that the correct quantum group analogue of the fixed-point subalgebra in the Letzter-Kolb theory is not a fixed-point subalgebra itself, but merely tends to one as
q\to 1
, see
[
Le99
, Sec. 4]
and
[
Ko14
, Ch. 10]
.
This suggests that there may be a generalization of this theory that does not require a fixed-point subalgebra as input.

A careful analysis of
[
Ko14
,
BK15
,
BK19
]
indeed indicates that the compatibility conditions for
X
and
\tau
can be weakened.
Indeed, in
[
BK15
, Rmks. 2.6 and 3.14]
it is explicitly suggested that some key passages of the theory are amenable for generalizations.
This leads to the notion of a
generalized Satake diagram
, see Definition
1
, and the whole theory survives in this setting with minor adjustments.
The resulting Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}(X,\tau)
is given in Definition
2
and the corresponding coideal subalgebra
B=B(X,\tau)
in Definition
4
.
For
\mathfrak{g}
of type A, all generalized Satake diagrams are Satake diagrams.
For other
\mathfrak{g}
, the generalized Satake diagrams that are not Satake diagrams are listed in Table
1
.

Our proposed generalization of Satake diagrams can be traced back to the work of A. Heck
[
He84
]
.
These diagrams classify involutions of the root system of
\mathfrak{g}
such that the corresponding restricted Weyl group is the Weyl group of the restricted root system.
The characterization in terms of the restricted Weyl group is relevant in the context of the universal R- and K-matrices for quantum symmetric pairs.
The universal R-matrix
\mathcal{R}
has a distinguished factor called quasi R-matrix playing an important role in the theory of canonical bases for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, see
[
Ka90
]
and
[
Lu94
, Part IV]
.
The quasi R-matrix possesses a remarkable factorization property expressed in terms of the braid group action on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
, see
[
KR90
,
LS90
]
.
Recently it has become clear that many of these properties extend to the universal K-matrix
\mathcal{K}
.
It has a distinguished factor called quasi K-matrix, introduced in
[
BW18a
]
for certain coideal subalgebras of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
and in a more general setting in
[
BK15
]
.
This object plays a prominent role in the theory of canonical bases for quantum symmetric pairs
[
BW18b
]
; for a historical note we refer the reader to
[
BW18b
, Rmk. 4.9]
.
In
[
DK19
]
a factorization property is established for the quasi K-matrix using a braid group action of the restricted Weyl group.
As a consequence of the present work, this factorization property naturally extends to quasi K-matrices defined in terms of generalized Satake diagrams.

The Kac-Moody generalization of this approach will be addressed in a future work.
Another outstanding issue is a Lie-theoretic motivation of the subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
, which we define in an
ad hoc
manner directly in terms of the combinatorial data
(X,\tau)
, see Definition
2
.
Therefore we now provide a further motivation for the study of the subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
and its quantization
B
.

1.1.
Some remarks on the representation theory of
(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),B)

Consider the completion
\mathcal{U}
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
with respect to the category of integrable
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules, so that objects in them have well-defined images under any finite-dimensional representation, see e.g.
[
Lu94
,
Jan96
]
.
Then
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{U}
can be embedded in a completion
\mathcal{U}^{(2)}
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes 2}
and one can construct an invertible
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{U}^{(2)}
satisfying

\mathcal{R}\Delta(a)=\Delta^{\rm op}(a)\mathcal{R}\text{ for all }a\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R})=R_{13}R_{23},\qquad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)(\mathcal{R})=R_{13}R_{12},

where
\Delta
is the coproduct and
\Delta^{\rm op}
the opposite coproduct (these can be viewed as maps from
\mathcal{U}
to
\mathcal{U}^{(2)}
).
Analogously, according to
[
BK19
,
Ko20
]
, one can construct an invertible
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{U}
and an involutive Hopf algebra automorphism
\phi
of
\mathcal{U}
such that
(\phi\otimes\phi)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}
and

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}b=\phi(b)\mathcal{K}\qquad\text{for all }b\in B,

(1.2)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}^{\phi})_{21}\mathcal{K}_{2}\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{B}^{(2)},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{R}_{21}(1\otimes\mathcal{K})\mathcal{R}^{\phi}(\mathcal{K}\otimes 1),

where
\mathcal{R}^{\phi}=(\phi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{R})
, the subscript
21
denotes the simple transposition of tensor factors in
\mathcal{U}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{B}^{(2)}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{(2)}
is a particular completion of
B\otimes U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
, see
[
Ko20
, Eq. (3.31)]
.
As a consequence, the
(universal)
\phi
-twisted reflection equation
is satisfied:

(1.4)

\mathcal{R}_{21}\,(1\otimes\mathcal{K})\,\mathcal{R}^{\phi}\,(\mathcal{K}\otimes 1)=(\mathcal{K}\otimes 1)\,(\mathcal{R}^{\phi})_{21}\,(1\otimes\mathcal{K})\,\mathcal{R}\qquad\in\mathcal{U}^{(2)}.

The automorphism
\phi
is given by
\tau\tau_{0}
where
\tau_{0}
is the diagram automorphism corresponding to the longest element of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
.
The expression for
\mathcal{K}
is given in
[
BK19
, Cor. 7.7]
.

One could argue in favour of making the automorphism
\phi
inner: adjoin to
\mathcal{U}
a group-like element
c_{\phi}
such that
\phi(u)=c_{\phi}uc_{\phi}^{-1}
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
.
Then the object
\mathcal{K}_{\phi}:=c_{\phi}^{-1}\mathcal{K}
satisfies
(
1.1
-
1.3
)
with
\phi
replaced by
\mathrm{id}
.
However, for certain nontrivial diagram automorphisms
\phi
,
c_{\phi}
cannot be chosen inside
\mathcal{U}
so that
\mathcal{K}_{\phi}
cannot be evaluated in all finite-dimensional representations.
This relates to the fact that the weights defining certain fundamental representations are not fixed by
\phi
.
For instance, if
\rho
is the vector representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
with
N>2
one checks that the matrices
\rho(\phi(u))
and
\rho(u)
are not simultaneously similar for all
u\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Now let
\rho
the vector representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
; if
\mathfrak{g}
is of exceptional type by this we mean the smallest fundamental representation (for
{\rm E}_{6}
one has a choice of two representations).
Choose
R\in\mathrm{GL}(V\otimes V)
proportional to
(\rho\otimes\rho)(\mathcal{R})
,
R^{\phi}\in\mathrm{GL}(V\otimes V)
proportional to
(\rho\otimes\rho)(\mathcal{R}^{\phi})
and
K\in\mathrm{GL}(V)
proportional to
\rho(\mathcal{K})
.
Applying
\rho\otimes\rho
to (
1.4
) one obtains the
matrix reflection equation

(1.5)

R_{21}\,(\mathrm{Id}\otimes K)\,R^{\phi}\,(K\otimes\mathrm{Id})=(K\otimes\mathrm{Id})\,(R^{\phi})_{21}\,(\mathrm{Id}\otimes K)\,R\qquad\in\mathrm{End}(V\otimes V)

where the subscript
21
indicates conjugation by the permutation operator in
\mathrm{GL}(V\otimes V)
.
Starting with
\mathfrak{g}
of classical Lie type and a coideal subalgebra
B=B(X,\tau)
where
(X,\tau)
is a Satake diagram, the matrices
\rho(\mathcal{K})
correspond to the solutions of (
1.5
) used in
[
NDS95
,
NS95
]
to define quantum symmetric pairs.

Treating the matrix
R
as given, one can of course solve (
1.5
) for
K\in\mathrm{GL}(V)
.
For
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
and
V=\mathbb{C}^{N}
this was done by A. Mudrov
[
Mu02
]
.
From this result and computations for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
whose vector representation is of dimension at most 9 (i.e. with
\mathfrak{g}
of types
{\rm B}_{n}
,
{\rm C}_{n}
,
{\rm D}_{n}
(
n\leq 4
) and
{\rm G}_{2}
) one obtains a classification of solutions
K
of (
1.5
) for those pairs
(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}),\rho)
.
One can match this list of solutions
K
one-to-one with a list of generalized Satake diagrams
(X,\tau)
by checking which
K
satisfies
K\rho(b)=\rho(\phi(b))K
for all
b\in B=B(X,\tau)
, i.e. the image of (
1.1
) under
\rho
.
Although this intertwining equation does not determine
K
uniquely, it turns out that, provided
K\notin\mathbb{C}\,\mathrm{Id}
, each
K
intertwines
\rho|_{B}
for a unique
B=B(X,\tau)
with
X
not equal to the whole Dynkin diagram
I
.
In the case
X=I
we must have
\tau=\tau_{0}
and
B=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
so that the excluded case
K\in\mathbb{C}\,\mathrm{Id}
can be matched to it.
It leads to the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1
.

Let
\rho:U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathrm{End}(V)
be the vector representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

(i)

If
K\in\mathrm{GL}(V)
is a solution of (
1.5
) there exists a generalized Satake diagram
(X,\tau)
such that
K
is proportional to
\rho(\mathcal{K})
where
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(X,\tau)
is the universal K-matrix for the subalgebra
B=B(X,\tau)
.

(ii)

The only quasitriangular coideal subalgebras of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
are of the form
(B(X,\tau),\mathcal{K}(X,\tau))
with
(X,\tau)
a generalized Satake diagram.

In the Letzter-Kolb approach, the generators of the coideal subalgebra
B
associated to a node
i\in I\backslash X
carry extra parameters: scalars
\gamma_{i}\neq 0
and
\sigma_{i}
, see Definition
4
and we can sharpen Conjecture
1
(i).
Namely, let
d_{i}
denote the squared length of root
\alpha_{i}
and write
q_{i}=q^{d_{i}}
.
Consider the set
I_{\rm ns}=\{i\in I\backslash X\,|\,i\text{ does not neighbour }X,\,\tau(i)=i\}
, see (
3.24
), and the sets
\Gamma_{q}
and
\Sigma_{q}
, see (
4.5
); these definition go back to
[
Le03
,
Ko14
]
.
Conjecturally, any invertible matrix solution
K
of (
1.5
) is proportional to
\rho(\mathcal{K})
for some
B(X,\tau)
with
(X,\tau)
a generalized Satake diagram whose parameters satisfy
(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I\backslash X}\in\Gamma_{q}
,
\sigma_{i}=0
if
i\notin I_{\rm ns}
and for all
(i,j)\in I_{\rm ns}\times I_{\rm ns}
such that
i\neq j
one of three conditions must hold: the Cartan integer
a_{ij}
is even,
\sigma_{j}=0
, or
(q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1})^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=-(q_{i}^{r/2}+q_{i}^{-r/2})^{2}q_{i}\gamma_{i}
for some odd positive
r\leq-a_{ij}
.
The set
\Sigma_{q}
does not cover the third possibility, which appeared in
[
BB10
]
for
a_{ij}\in\{-1,-3\}
.
Conjecture
1
(ii) can be made more precise in an analogous way.

The approach in
[
BK19
]
requires also certain constraints on
\gamma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}
under the transformation
q\to q^{-1}
which are given in (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) in the present notation and generality.

1.2.
Outline

The paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
we define the basic objects associated to a finite-dimensional semisimple complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and its Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
.
We introduce generalized Satake diagrams and explain how they emerge in the work of A. Heck.

In Section
3
we define the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
in terms of
(X,\tau)
.
In Theorem
3.1
, the main result of this section, we show that
\mathfrak{k}
satisfies
\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
precisely if
(X,\tau)
is a generalized Satake diagram.
In Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
we describe the derived subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
and establish that when
\mathfrak{k}
is not reductive it is a semidirect product of a reductive subalgebra and a nilpotent ideal of class 2.
We end this section discussing the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{k})
.

In Section
4
we indicate the necessary modifications to the papers
[
Ko14
,
BK15
,
BK19
,
Ko20
,
DK19
]
so that they apply to the quantum pair algebras
B=U_{q}(\mathfrak{k})
associated to generalized Satake diagrams.

Appendix
A
contains three technical lemmas in aid of Section
3
.

We use the symbol
\diameter
to indicate the end of definitions, examples and remarks.

2.
Finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebras and root system involutions

Let
I
be a finite set and
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
a Cartan matrix.
In particular, there exist positive rationals
d_{i}
(
i\in I
) such that
d_{i}a_{ij}=d_{j}a_{ji}
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}(A)
be the corresponding finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
.
It is generated by
\{e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}\}_{i\in I}
subject to

(2.1)

\displaystyle[h_{i},h_{j}]=0,\qquad[h_{i},e_{j}]=a_{ij}e_{j},\qquad[h_{i},f_{j}]=-a_{ij}f_{j},\qquad[e_{i},f_{j}]=\delta_{ij}h_{i},

(2.2)

\displaystyle{\rm ad}(e_{i})^{M_{ij}}(e_{j})={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{M_{ij}}(f_{j})=0\qquad\text{if }i\neq j,

for all
i,j\in I
, where we have set
M_{ij}:=1-a_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
if
i\neq j
.
The standard Cartan and nilpotent subalgebras are
\mathfrak{h}=\langle h_{i}\,|\,i\in I\rangle
,
\mathfrak{n}^{+}=\langle e_{i}\,|\,i\in I\rangle
and
\mathfrak{n}^{-}=\langle f_{i}\,|\,i\in I\rangle
.

The simple roots
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
(
i\in I
) satisfy
\alpha_{j}(h_{i})=a_{ij}
for
i,j\in I
.
Let
Q=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
denote the root lattice and write
Q^{+}=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
.
For all
\alpha,\beta\in Q
, we write
\alpha>\beta
if
\alpha-\beta\in Q^{+}\backslash\{0\}
.
The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is
Q
-graded in terms of the root spaces
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\,|\,[h,x]=\alpha(h)\,x\text{ for all }h\in\mathfrak{h}\}
and we have the following identities for
\mathfrak{h}
-modules:

(2.3)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-},\qquad\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha},\qquad\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}.

Hence the root system
\Phi:=\{\alpha\in Q\,|\,\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\neq\{0\},\,\alpha\neq 0\}
satisfies
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi^{-}
where
\Phi^{\pm}=\pm(\Phi\cap Q^{+})
.
The Weyl group
W
is the (finite) subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{h}^{*})
generated by the simple reflections
s_{i}
(
i\in I
) acting via
s_{i}(\alpha)=\alpha-\alpha(h_{i})\,\alpha_{i}
for all
i\in I
,
\alpha\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
.
We define

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\rm Aut}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathfrak{h}^{*})\,|\,g(\Phi)=\Phi\},

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\rm Aut}(A)

\displaystyle=\{\sigma:I\to I\text{ invertible}\,\,|\,\,a_{\sigma(i)\,\sigma(j)}=a_{ij}\text{ for all }i,j\in I\}.

Then
{\rm Aut}(\Phi)=W\rtimes{\rm Aut}(A)
, with
{\rm Aut}(A)
acting by relabelling.

We briefly review some important subgroups of

(2.6)

{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})=\{\sigma\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g})\,|\,\sigma(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{h}\}<{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g}).

We have
{\rm Aut}(A)<{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
(acting by relabelling).
Also, a braid group action on
\mathfrak{g}
is given by
{\rm Ad}(s_{i})=\exp({\rm ad}(e_{i}))\exp({\rm ad}(-f_{i}))\exp({\rm ad}(e_{i}))\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
for
i\in I
.
It extends the action of
W
on
\mathfrak{h}
dual to the one on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and satisfies
{\rm Ad}(W)<{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
.
The Chevalley involution
\omega\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
is defined by swapping
e_{i}
and
-f_{i}
for all
i\in I
; it commutes with
{\rm Ad}(W)
and with
{\rm Aut}(A)
.
Finally, the group
\widetilde{H}:=\mathrm{Hom}(Q,\mathbb{C}^{\times})
naturally induces a subgroup
{\rm Ad}(\widetilde{H})<{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
via
{\rm Ad}(\chi)|_{\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}=\chi(\alpha)\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}
for all
\chi\in\widetilde{H}
,
\alpha\in Q
.

The elements of
{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
can be dualized to elements of
{\rm Aut}(\Phi)
.
Conversely, since
-\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}\in{\rm Aut}(\Phi)
and
{\rm Aut}(\Phi)=W\rtimes{\rm Aut}(A)
, given
g\in{\rm Aut}(\Phi)
there exists a unique
(w,\tau)\in W\times{\rm Aut}(A)
such that
g=-w\tau
.
Then
\psi={\rm Ad}(w)\,\omega\tau\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
satisfies
(\psi|_{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}=g
.

2.1.
Compatible decorations and involutions of
\Phi

Given a subset
X\subseteq I
denote the corresponding Cartan submatrix by
A_{X}=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in X}
and consider the semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{X}:=\langle e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}\,|\,i\in X\rangle\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
with Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{X}=\mathfrak{h}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{X}
and dual Weyl vector
\rho^{\vee}_{X}\in\mathfrak{h}_{X}
.
The unique longest element
w_{X}
of the Weyl group
W_{X}:=\langle s_{i}\,|\,i\in X\rangle
is an involution and there exists
\tau_{0,X}\in{\rm Aut}(A_{X})
which satisfies

(2.7)

-w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{\tau_{0,X}(i)}\qquad\text{for all }i\in X.

We recall here the basic fact that both
w_{X}
and
\tau_{0,X}
naturally factorize with respect to the decomposition of
X
into connected components.
Furthermore, if
X
is connected then
\tau_{0,X}
is trivial unless
X
is of type
{\rm A}_{n}
with
n>1
,
{\rm D}_{n}
with
n>4
odd or
{\rm E}_{6}
(in each case of which there is a unique nontrivial diagram automorphism).
Note that
{\rm Ad}(w_{X})|_{\mathfrak{g}_{X}}=\tau_{0,X}\,\omega|_{\mathfrak{g}_{X}}
and
{\rm Ad}(w_{X})^{2}={\rm Ad}(\zeta)
, where
\zeta\in\widetilde{H}
is defined by
\zeta(\alpha)=(-1)^{\alpha(2\rho^{\vee}_{X})}
for all
\alpha\in Q
.

We can describe

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})

\displaystyle:=\{\psi\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})\,\,|\,\,\psi^{2}|_{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{h}}\},

(2.9)

\displaystyle{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\Phi)

\displaystyle:=\{g\in{\rm Aut}(\Phi)\,\,|\,\,g^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}\}

by combinatorial data.
Define the set of
compatible decorations
as

(2.10)

\mathrm{CDec}(A)=\{(X,\tau)\,\,|\,\,X\subseteq I,\,\tau\in{\rm Aut}(A),\,\tau^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{I},\,\tau(X)=X,\,\tau|_{X}=\tau_{0,X}\}.

In the associated Dynkin diagram one marks a compatible decoration by filling the nodes corresponding to
X
and drawing bidirectional arrows for the nontrivial orbits of
\tau
.

Example 1
.

Let
A
be of type
{\rm A}_{n}
,
n\geq 2
.
The compatible decorations are

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{k}

r

r

where
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
,
p_{1},p_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
p_{2},\dots,p_{k-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
r
, the number of
\tau
-orbits in
X
, is constrained by
0\leq r\leq\lceil n/2\rceil
.

\diameter

Given
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
, we define

(2.11)

\theta=\theta(X,\tau)=-w_{X}\tau\in{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\Phi).

As explained above, the map dual to
\theta
can be extended to an element of
{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, also called
\theta
and given by
\theta={\rm Ad}(w_{X})\,\tau\omega
.
Owing to aforementioned properties of
{\rm Ad}(w_{X})
we have

(2.12)

\theta|_{\mathfrak{g}_{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{X}},\qquad\theta^{2}={\rm Ad}(\zeta).

Note that
(\mathrm{id}-\theta)(h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)})
lies in
\mathfrak{h}_{X}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
for all
i\in I
.
Hence it vanishes so that

(2.13)

\theta(h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)})=h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}.

We fix a subset
I^{*}\subseteq I\backslash X
containing precisely one element from each
\tau
-orbit in
I\backslash X
.
As a consequence of (
2.13
) we have

(2.14)

\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}=\bigoplus_{i\in X}\mathbb{C}h_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I^{*}\\
i\neq\tau(i)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{C}(h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}).

2.2.
Generalized Satake diagrams and the restricted Weyl group

For
i\in I\backslash X
denote by
\check{X}(i)
the union of connected components of
X
neighbouring
\{i,\tau(i)\}
.

Definition 1
.

Generalized Satake diagrams
are elements of the set

\displaystyle\mathrm{GSat}(A):=\Big\{(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)\,\,\Big|\,\,\forall i\in I\backslash X:\,\check{X}(i)\cup\{i,\tau(i)\}\neq\hbox to15.64pt{\vbox to4.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.18571pt\lower-2.48407pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 14.76 0 L 29.53 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 14.76 0 M 17.72 0 C 17.72 1.63 16.39 2.95 14.76 2.95 C 13.13 2.95 11.81 1.63 11.81 0 C 11.81 -1.63 13.13 -2.95 14.76 -2.95 C 16.39 -2.95 17.72 -1.63 17.72 0 Z M 14.76 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 29.53 0 M 32.48 0 C 32.48 1.63 31.16 2.95 29.53 2.95 C 27.9 2.95 26.57 1.63 26.57 0 C 26.57 -1.63 27.9 -2.95 29.53 -2.95 C 31.16 -2.95 32.48 -1.63 32.48 0 Z M 29.53 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Big\}.

\displaystyle\diameter

The compatible decorations considered in Example
1
lie in
\mathrm{GSat}(A)
if and only if
p_{1}=p_{k}=0
and
p_{2}=\ldots=p_{k-1}=1
.

Remark 1
.

Generalized Satake diagrams were first considered by A. Heck in
[
He84
]
.
He uses the term “Satake diagrams” in a more general setting, see
[
He84
, §1 - §2]
: he starts with
\sigma=-\theta\in{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\Phi)
and calls a base
\Pi
of
\Phi

\sigma
-fundamental if for all
\alpha\in\Pi
either
\theta(\alpha)=\alpha
or
\theta(\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\Pi
.
Letting
X
consist of the nodes corresponding to
\Pi^{\theta}
in the Dynkin diagram corresponding to
\Pi
, it follows that
\tau:=\sigma w_{X}
is an involutive diagram automorphism restricting on
X
to
\tau_{0,X}
.
He calls
(X,\tau)
the Satake diagram of
\sigma
, which we call a compatible decoration; what he calls an “admissible Satake diagram” is in our case a generalized Satake diagram.
Since the term “Satake diagram” has come to be associated to involutions of the complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we prefer the nomenclature “compatible decoration” and “generalized Satake diagram”.

\diameter

Note that
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
is a generalized Satake diagram precisely if

(2.15)

\forall(i,j)\in I\backslash X\times X\,:\,\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}\,\implies\,a_{ij}\neq-1,

which is the condition needed in
[
Ko14
, Proof of Lemma 5.11, Step 1]
and
[
BK19
, Proof of Lemma 6.4]
.
Straightforwardly one checks that it is equivalent to any of the following conditions:

(2.16)

\displaystyle\forall(i,j)\in I\backslash X\times X\,:\,\tau(i)=i,\,X(i)=\{j\}\,\implies a_{ij}a_{ji}\neq 1,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\forall i,j\in I:\,\theta(\alpha_{i})=-(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j})\,\implies\,a_{ij}\neq-1,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\forall i\in I:\,(\theta(\alpha_{i}))(h_{i})\neq-1.

Satake diagrams can be defined as the following subset of compatible decorations of
A
:

(2.19)

\mathrm{Sat}(A)=\{(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)\,\,|\,\,\forall i\in I\backslash X:\,i=\tau(i)\,\implies\,\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Satake diagrams classify involutive Lie algebra automorphisms up to conjugacy, see e.g.
[
Ar62
]
.
In our notation, for
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{I^{*}}
define
\chi_{\bm{\gamma}}\in\widetilde{H}
and
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
by

(2.20)

\chi_{\bm{\gamma}}(\alpha_{i})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i\in X,\\
\gamma_{i}&\text{if }i\in I^{*},\\
\gamma_{\tau(i)}\zeta(\alpha_{i})&\text{if }i\in I\backslash(X\cup\backslash I^{*}),\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\qquad\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}={\rm Ad}(\chi_{\bm{\gamma}})\,\theta

and note that (
2.12
) implies
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}
if
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
.

We have
\mathrm{Sat}(A)\subseteq\mathrm{GSat}(A)
; indeed, if
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)\backslash\mathrm{GSat}(A)
there exists
(i,j)\in I\backslash X\times X
such that
\tau(i)=i
,
\check{X}(i)=\{j\}
and
a_{ij}=a_{ji}=-1
, so that
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})=a_{ji}/2\notin\mathbb{Z}
implying
(X,\tau)\notin\mathrm{Sat}(A)
.
We refer the reader to the classification of generalized Satake diagrams in
[
He84
, Table I]
.
Since this does not distinguish between elements of
\mathrm{Sat}(A)
and
\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
, for convenience we list the elements of
\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
, see Table
1
; note that outside type
{\rm A}_{n}
we have
\mathrm{GSat}(A)\neq\mathrm{Sat}(A)
.

Table 1.
The set
\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
for indecomposable Cartan matrices
A
.
By a case-by-case analysis there is a unique
i\in I
such that
i=\tau(i)
and
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\notin\mathbb{Z}
and we have indicated that node in the diagrams.
The classical families of diagrams are labelled in the standard way.
For types
{\rm C}_{n}
and
{\rm D}_{n}
upper bounds on
i
are imposed to avoid the cases when
\theta
is an involution whose fixed-point subalgebra is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
.

.
For fixed
\theta\in{\rm Aut}^{\rm inv}(\Phi)
we have the decomposition
V=V^{\theta}\oplus V^{-\theta}
.
Denote by
\overline{\>\>\vphantom{\alpha}}:V\to V
the corresponding projection onto
V^{-\theta}
.
Now consider the
restricted Weyl group
and the set of
restricted roots

(2.21)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{W}=\{w|_{V^{-\theta}}\,\,|\,\,w\in W,\,w(V^{-\theta})\subseteq V^{-\theta}\},\qquad\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\Phi}=\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\alpha}\,\,|\,\,\alpha\in\Phi\}\backslash\{0\}.

If
\theta=\theta(X,\tau)
with
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
then
W_{X}
is a normal subgroup of
W^{\theta}=\{w\in W\,|\,\theta w=w\theta\}
.
By
[
He84
, Prop. 3.1]
we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{W}\cong W^{\theta}/W_{X}
.
For
i\in I^{*}
denote
X[i]=X\cup\{i,\tau(i)\}
and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{s}_{i}\in\mathrm{GL}(V^{-\theta})
be the element that sends
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\alpha}_{i}
to
-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\alpha}_{i}
and fixes all
\beta\in V^{-\theta}
with
\beta(h_{i})=0
.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
He84
]
and
[
Lu76
]
)
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

We have
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
.

(ii)

For all
i\in I^{*}
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{s}_{i}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{W}
.

(iii)

For all
i\in I^{*}
,
\widetilde{s}_{i}:=w_{X}w_{X[i]}
lies in
W^{\theta}
and satisfies
\widetilde{s}_{i}|_{V^{-\theta}}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{s}_{i}
.

(iv)

For all
i\in I^{*}
,
\tau_{0,X[i]}
preserves
X
.

(v)

The restricted Weyl group
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{W}
is the Weyl group of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\Phi}
.

(vi)

The set
\{\widetilde{s}_{i}\,|\,i\in I^{*}\}
is a Coxeter system for the group it generates.

Proof.

The equivalence of the statements (ii), (iii), (iv) and (v) is shown in
[
He84
, Lemma 3.2, Thm. 3.3, Thm. 4.4]
.
The implication (iv)
\implies
(vi) is shown in
[
Lu76
, 5.9 (i)]
(also see
[
Lu02
, 25.1]
).
Its converse follows by noting that if (iv) fails then for some
i\in I^{*}
,
w_{X[i]}
and
w_{X}
do not commute so that
\widetilde{s}_{i}^{2}\neq\mathrm{id}_{V}
.
Finally, to show (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv), note that by factorizability of
\tau_{0,X[i]}
over connected components of
X[i]
, without loss of generality we may restrict to the case where
X[i]
is connected and equals
I
.
Since there is nothing to prove if
\tau_{0,X[i]}=\mathrm{id}
, it remains to check the cases where
X[i]
is of type
{\rm A}_{n}
with
n>1
,
{\rm D}_{n}
with
n>4
odd or
{\rm E}_{6}
.
Classifying all
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
such that
I=X[i]
for some
i\in I^{*}
,
I
is connected and
\tau_{0,X[i]}\neq\mathrm{id}
we obtain the following diagrams in the top row:

The bottom row shows the corresponding compatible decoration
(X[i],\tau_{0,X[i]})
.
The first case is not in
\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and for the remaining six cases the subset
X
is preserved by
\tau_{0,X[i]}
.
∎

Remark 2
.

Note that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\Phi}
is not always a root system.
By
[
He84
, Thm. 6.1]
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\Phi}
is a (possibly non-reduced or empty) root system precisely if
\tau_{0,X[i]}
preserves
X
for all
i\in I^{*}
or
(X,\tau)=\hbox to15.64pt{\vbox to4.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.18571pt\lower-2.48407pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

.

\diameter

3.
The subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}

For
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
the subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
can be described in terms of generators; see e.g.
[
Ko14
, Lemma 2.8]
.
This motivates the following more general definition.

Definition 2
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
.
For
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
let
\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}}=\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}}(X,\tau)
be the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{X}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
and, for all
i\in I\backslash X
,

(3.1)

\displaystyle b_{i;\gamma_{i}}:=f_{i}+\gamma_{i}\,\theta(f_{i}).

\displaystyle\diameter

It is convenient to suppress the dependence on
\bm{\gamma}
and simply write
b_{i}
and
\mathfrak{k}
if there is no cause for confusion.
We denote
b_{i}=f_{i}
if
i\in X
.
Note that
\mathfrak{h}_{X}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
.
It follows that
\mathfrak{k}
is generated by
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}:=\{e_{i}\,|\,i\in X\}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
and
b_{i}
for
i\in I
.
Owing to
(
2.1
-
2.2
)
and (
2.12
), these satisfy

(3.2)

\displaystyle[e_{i},b_{j}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}h_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\text{for all }i\in X,\,j\in I,

(3.3)

\displaystyle[h,b_{j}]

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{j}(h)\,b_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for all }h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta},\,j\in I,

(3.4)

\displaystyle[h,e_{j}]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{j}(h)\,e_{j}

\displaystyle\text{for all }h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta},\,j\in X,

(3.5)

\displaystyle[h,h^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for all }h,h^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta},

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\rm ad}(e_{i})^{1-a_{ij}}(e_{j})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for all }i,j\in X,\,i\neq j.

In Appendix
A
we study the repeated adjoint action of
b_{i}
on
b_{j}
for
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
.
By setting
m=M_{ij}
in Lemmas
(
A.1
-
A.3
)
one obtains the following Serre-type relations for the generators
b_{i}
:

(3.7)

\hskip-3.0pt{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}(1+\zeta(\alpha_{i}))\,\gamma_{i}\,[\theta(f_{i}),[f_{i},f_{j}]]\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}\hskip-5.0pt&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{-},\,a_{ij}=-1,\\
-18\gamma_{i}^{2}\,e_{j}&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}=0,\,a_{ij}=-3,\\
-\gamma_{i}\,(2h_{i}+h_{j})&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}=0,\,a_{ij}=-1,\\
\big(\gamma_{i}+\zeta(\alpha_{i})\gamma_{j}\big)\,[\theta(f_{i}),f_{j}]\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}\hskip-5.0pt&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{-},\,a_{ij}=0,\\
\gamma_{j}h_{i}-\gamma_{i}\,h_{j}&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{j}=0,\,a_{ij}=0,\\
2\,(\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{j})\,b_{i}&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{j}=0,\,a_{ij}=-1,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\big\lfloor\tfrac{M_{ij}}{2}\big\rfloor}p_{ij}^{(r,M_{ij})}\gamma_{i}^{r}{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}-2r}(b_{j})\hskip-5.0pt&\text{if }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}=0,\,j\in I\backslash X,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\hskip-5.0pt

For
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
and
m,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have defined
p_{ij}^{(r,m)}\in\mathbb{Z}
by setting
p_{ij}^{(r,m)}=0
if
r>\lfloor m/2\rfloor
,
p_{ij}^{(0,m)}=-1
and

(3.8)

p_{ij}^{(r,m)}=p_{ij}^{(r,m-1)}+(m-1)(M_{ij}+1-m)\,p_{ij}^{(r-1,m-2)}\qquad\text{if }0<r\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor.

By induction with respect to
m
, it can be shown that these integers satisfy

(3.9)

p_{ij}^{(r,m)}<0\qquad\text{if }0\leq 2r\leq m\leq M_{ij}.

Indeed, (
3.9
) is true for
m\in\{0,1\}
.
Suppose
0\leq 2r\leq m
and
1<m\leq M_{ij}
and assume (
3.9
) holds with
m
replaced by
m-1
and by
m-2
.
If
p_{ij}^{(r,m-1)}=0
we must have
r=m/2
so that
p_{ij}^{(r-1,m-2)}<0
.
Hence at least one of
p_{ij}^{(r,m-1)},p_{ij}^{(r-1,m-2)}
is nonzero and the observation that
(m-1)(M_{ij}+1-m)>0
completes the induction step.

As
\mathfrak{g}
is of finite type,
M_{ij}\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
Hence the penultimate case of (
3.7
) amounts to

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }a_{ij}=0\\
-\gamma_{i}b_{j}&\text{if }a_{ij}=-1\\
-4\gamma_{i}\,[b_{i},b_{j}]&\text{if }a_{ij}=-2\\
-9\gamma_{i}^{2}b_{j}-10\gamma_{i}\,[b_{i},[b_{i},b_{j}]]&\text{if }a_{ij}=-3\end{cases}\quad\text{and }\theta(\alpha_{i})+\alpha_{i}=0,\,j\in I\backslash X.

Remark 3
.

(i)

Definition
2
can be used in the general Kac-Moody case, so that
(
3.2
-
3.6
)
still hold.
Also the results of Appendix
A
are valid in this general setting and hence so is (
3.7
).
We will discuss the subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}(X,\tau)
in the Kac-Moody setting in future work.

(ii)

The relations (
3.7
) entail that
{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}}(b_{j})=0
if
i\in X
or if
\tau(i)\notin\{i,j\}
as required by the specialization of
[
Ko14
, Eq. (5.20) and Thm. 7.3]
.

\diameter

3.1.
Basic structure of
\mathfrak{k}

From now on we assume that the
\gamma_{i}
are nonzero.
In order to state the main result of this section, we need some notation.
For all
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
denote
\lambda_{ij}:=M_{ij}\,\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}\in Q^{+}\backslash\Phi
and consider the sets

(3.10)

\displaystyle I_{\rm diff}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I^{*}\,\,|\,\,i\neq\tau(i)\text{ and }(\theta(\alpha_{i}))(h_{i})\neq 0\}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I^{*}\,\,|\,\,i\neq\tau(i)\text{ and }\exists j\in X[i]{\rm\,\;s.t.\;}a_{ij}<0\}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=\Gamma(X,\tau)=\{\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{I\backslash X}\,\,|\,\,\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{\tau(i)}\text{ if }i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm diff}\}.

For
\bm{i}\in I^{\ell}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we write
\alpha_{\bm{i}}=\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{i_{r}}
and

b_{\bm{i}}={\rm ad}(b_{i_{1}})\cdots{\rm ad}(b_{i_{\ell-1}})(b_{i_{\ell}}),\quad e_{\bm{i}}={\rm ad}(e_{i_{1}})\cdots{\rm ad}(e_{i_{\ell-1}})(e_{i_{\ell}}),\quad f_{\bm{i}}={\rm ad}(f_{i_{1}})\cdots{\rm ad}(f_{i_{\ell-1}})(f_{i_{\ell}}).

Observe that

\mathfrak{n}^{-}=\mathrm{Sp}\{f_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in I^{\ell},\,\ell>0\},\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}=\mathrm{Sp}\{e_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in X^{\ell},\,\ell>0\}.

Hence for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we can choose
\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\subseteq I^{\ell}
such that
\{f_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\ell}}
is a basis for
\mathrm{Sp}\{f_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in I^{\ell}}
and
\{e_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{X,\ell}}
is a basis for
\mathrm{Sp}\{e_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in X^{\ell}}
where
\mathcal{J}_{X,\ell}:=\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\cap X^{\ell}
.
Let

(3.12)

\mathcal{J}:=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathcal{J}_{\ell},\qquad\qquad\mathcal{J}_{X}:=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathcal{J}_{X,\ell}.

Then
\{f_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
and
\{e_{\bm{i}}\}_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{X}}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{I\backslash X}
.
The following statements are equivalent:

(i)

We have
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

For all
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
we have

(3.13)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}}(b_{j})\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}\oplus\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\bigoplus_{\bm{k}\in I^{\ell},\,\alpha_{\bm{k}}<\lambda_{ij}}\mathbb{C}b_{\bm{k}}.

(iii)

We have the following identity for
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
-modules:

(3.14)

\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}\oplus\bigoplus_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{C}b_{\bm{i}}.

(iv)

We have

(3.15)

\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}.

Proof.

(i)
\Longleftrightarrow
(ii):

This is a direct consequence of (
3.7
).

(ii)
\implies
(iii):

Owing to
(
3.3
-
3.5
)
it is sufficient to prove (
3.14
) as an identity for vector spaces.
First we prove that
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\mathrm{Sp}\{b_{\bm{i}}\,|\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}\}
.
From
(
3.2
-
3.3
)
it follows that

(3.16)

\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\langle b_{j}\rangle_{j\in I}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\sum_{\bm{i}\in I^{\ell}}\mathbb{C}b_{\bm{i}}

as vector spaces.
Hence it suffices to prove that for all
\bm{j}\in\cup_{\ell}I^{\ell}
we have

(3.17)

b_{\bm{j}}\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\mathrm{Sp}\{b_{\bm{i}}\,\,|\,\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}\}.

We will prove this by induction with respect to the height
\ell
.
Since for all
j\in I
we have
\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{j}})=1
and hence
(j)\in\mathcal{J}
, the case
\ell=1
is trivial.
Now fix
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
and assume that (
3.17
) holds true for all smaller positive integers.
Fix
\bm{j}\in I^{\ell}
and repeatedly apply the Serre relations (
2.2
) to obtain that for all
\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\ell}
there exist
a_{\bm{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
f_{\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\ell}}a_{\bm{i}}f_{\bm{i}}
.
Hence, by virtue of (ii) and equations
(
3.2
-
3.3
)
it follows that

(3.18)

b_{\bm{j}}-\sum_{\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\ell}}a_{\bm{i}}b_{\bm{i}}\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\mathrm{Sp}\bigg\{b_{\bm{i}}\,\bigg|\,\bm{i}\in\bigcup_{m=1}^{\ell-1}I^{m}\bigg\}.

Using the induction hypothesis for the elements
b_{\bm{i}}
in the last summation one obtains (
3.17
).
It remains to show that the sum in (
3.17
) is direct.
Let
\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}
.
Then
f_{\bm{j}}
is nonzero.
Because of the explicit formula (
3.1
) we have

(3.19)

b_{\bm{j}}-f_{\bm{j}}\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}+\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}+\mathbb{C}\theta(f_{\bm{j}})+\mathrm{Sp}\{b_{\bm{i}}\,\,|\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J},\,\alpha_{\bm{i}}<\alpha_{\bm{j}}\}.

Hence
f_{\bm{j}}=\pi_{-\alpha_{\bm{j}}}(b_{\bm{j}})
for all
\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\pi_{\alpha}
is the projection on
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
, see (
2.3
).
Thus the linear independence of
\{f_{\bm{j}}\}_{\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}}
together with (
2.3
) implies that the sum is direct.

(iii)
\implies
(iv):

By definition,
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}
so it suffices to show that
\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
.
Suppose
h\in\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
.
By
\pi_{-\alpha_{\bm{j}}}(b_{\bm{j}})=f_{\bm{j}}
and the triangular decomposition (
2.3
), part (iii) implies
h\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
so
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
.

(iv)
\implies
(ii):

We prove the contrapositive.
If (
3.13
) fails then (
3.7
) and (
2.14
) imply

(3.20)

\gamma_{j}h_{i}-\gamma_{i}h_{j}\in\mathfrak{k}\cap(\mathfrak{h}\backslash\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})\text{ with }\gamma_{i}\neq\gamma_{j}\qquad\text{or}\qquad 2h_{i}+h_{j}\in\mathfrak{k}\cap(\mathfrak{h}\backslash\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}).

In either case (
3.15
) does not hold.
∎

Note that if
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}}
then (
3.15
) is trivially satisfied, since
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}=\mathfrak{h}^{\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}}
.
Given
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
\mathcal{J}
defined by (
3.12
), from (
2.14
) and (
3.14
) we obtain the
standard basis
for
\mathfrak{k}
:

(3.21)

\{e_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{X}\}\;\cup\;\{h_{i}\,|\,i\in X\}\;\cup\;\{h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}\,|\,i\in I^{*},\,i\neq\tau(i)\}\;\cup\;\{b_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}\}.

We denote
\Phi_{X}=\Phi\cap Q_{X}
where
Q_{X}=\sum_{i\in X}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
.
Combining (
3.21
) with
|\mathcal{J}|=|\Phi|/2
and
\dim(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})=|I|-|I^{*}|
, itself a consequence of (
2.14
), it follows that

(3.22)

\dim(\mathfrak{k})=|\Phi_{X}|/2+|I|-|I^{*}|+|\Phi|/2.

J. Stokman showed in
[
St19
]
that
generalized Onsager algebra
, i.e. the Lie algebra with generators
\widetilde{b}_{i}
(
i\in I
) and relations

(3.23)

{\rm ad}(\widetilde{b}_{i})^{M_{ij}}(\widetilde{b}_{j})=\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor M_{ij}/2\rfloor}p_{ij}^{(r,M_{ij})}{\rm ad}(\widetilde{b}_{i})^{M_{ij}-2r}(\widetilde{b}_{j})

is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{k}_{(1,1\ldots,1)}(\emptyset,\mathrm{id})=\mathfrak{g}^{\omega}
via
\widetilde{b}_{i}\mapsto b_{i}=f_{i}+\omega(f_{i})=f_{i}-e_{i}
.
Without loss of generality we can set
\bm{\gamma}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
since
\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}}={\rm Ad}(\chi{(\gamma_{1}^{1/2},\gamma_{2}^{1/2},\ldots,\gamma_{|I|}^{1/2})})(\mathfrak{k}_{(1,1,\ldots,1)})
for all
\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{I\backslash X}
.
We now discuss a generalization of this to arbitrary
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
.

Conjecture 2
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
.
The Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{k}}
generated by symbols
\widetilde{h}_{i},\widetilde{e}_{i}
(
i\in X
),
\widetilde{h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}}
(
i\in I^{*},i\neq\tau(i)
),
\widetilde{b}_{i}
(
i\in I
) and the relations obtained from
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
by adding tildes appropriately is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{k}
.

The only obstacle to promoting this to a theorem, and thereby settling the question posed in
[
Ko14
, Rmk. 2.10]
, is the lack of a general explicit formula for the right-hand sides in (
3.7
) in terms of the
e_{k}
with
k\in X
, instead of
\theta(f_{i})
;
for individual choices of
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
these explicit expressions can be found, or such relations do not occur at all, and in those cases one could prove the statement in the conjecture as follows.
Because the generators of the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
satisfy
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
, one has a surjective Lie algebra homomorphism
\phi:\widetilde{\mathfrak{k}}\to\mathfrak{k}
defined on generators by removing the tilde.
On the other hand, in
\mathfrak{k}
there are no relations involving the
b_{i}
other than
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
, as otherwise applying the appropriate projection
\pi_{-\alpha}
onto
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
maximal would yield a relation involving the
f_{i}
other than those given in
(
2.1
-
2.2
)
.
From this one can deduce that
\phi
is injective and obtain the statement in Conjecture
2
.

3.2.
Semidirect product decompositions of
\mathfrak{k}

In this section we assume that
A
is indecomposable, so that
\mathfrak{g}
is simple.
In order to describe the derived subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
recall the set
I_{\rm diff}\in I^{*}
and define

(3.24)

\displaystyle I_{\rm ns}

\displaystyle=\{i\in I\,|\,(\theta(\alpha_{i}))(h_{i})=-2\}=\{i\in I\,|\,i=\tau(i),\,\check{X}(i)=\emptyset\}\subseteq I^{*},

\displaystyle I_{\rm nsf}

\displaystyle=\{j\in I_{\rm ns}\,|\,a_{ij}\in 2\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }i\in I_{\rm ns}\}.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
.
The set

\{e_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{X}\}\;\cup\;\{h_{i}\,|\,i\in X\}\;\cup\;\{h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}\,|\,i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm diff},\,i\neq\tau(i)\}\;\cup\;\{b_{\bm{i}}\,|\,\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J},\,\bm{i}\neq(j)\text{ with }j\in I_{\rm nsf}\}.

is a basis for the derived subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
and we have

(3.25)

\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\rtimes\Big(\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\rm diff}}\mathbb{C}(h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)})\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in I_{\rm nsf}}\mathbb{C}b_{j}\Big).

Proof.

Fix
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
.
Note that in
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
neither
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}
(
i\in I_{\rm diff}
) nor
b_{j}
(
j\in I_{\rm nsf}
) appears in the right-hand side.
From the decomposition (
3.14
) it follows that these elements are not linear combinations of Lie brackets in
\mathfrak{k}
.
It suffices to show that the remaining basis elements specified in (
3.21
) are linear combinations of Lie brackets in
\mathfrak{k}
.

•

For
b_{\bm{i}}
with
\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{\ell}
and
e_{\bm{i}}
with
\bm{i}\in\mathcal{J}_{X,\ell}
with
\ell>1
, this holds by definition.

•

For
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
,
h_{i}
with
i\in X
, this follows from
(
3.2
-
3.4
)
.

•

For
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}
with
i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm diff}
and
i\neq\tau(i)
, the constraint on
i
is equivalent to
w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}
and
a_{i\,\tau(i)}=0
.
Hence (
3.7
) implies that
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}=\gamma_{i}^{-1}[b_{i},b_{\tau(i)}]
.

•

For
b_{j}
with
X(j)\neq\emptyset
there exists
i\in X
such that
a_{ij}\neq 0
.
By (
3.3
) we have
b_{j}=-a_{ij}^{-1}[h_{i},b_{j}]
.

•

For
b_{j}
with
j\neq\tau(j)
, by (
3.3
) we have
b_{j}=(a_{\tau(j)\,j}-2)^{-1}[h_{j}-h_{\tau(j)},b_{j}]
.

•

For
b_{j}
with
j\in I_{\rm ns}\backslash I_{\rm nsf}
there exists
i\in I_{\rm ns}
such that
a_{ij}
is odd.
From (
3.7
) we deduce

(3.26)

p_{ij}^{(M_{ij},2M_{ij})}b_{j}=\gamma_{i}^{-M_{ij}}{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{2M_{ij}}(b_{j})-\sum_{r=1}^{M_{ij}-1}p_{ij}^{(r,2M_{ij})}\gamma_{i}^{r-M_{ij}}{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{2(M_{ij}-r)}(b_{j}).

By (
3.9
)
p_{ij}^{(M_{ij},2M_{ij})}
is nonzero.
∎

From Proposition
3.2
it follows that the codimension of
\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{k}
equals
|I_{\rm diff}|+|I_{\rm nsf}|
.
For
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
, in
[
Le02
, Sec. 7, Variation 1]
it was noted that
|I_{\rm diff}|\leq 1
if
A
is of finite type.
In light of the above it is natural to generalize this by involving the set
I_{\rm nsf}
and allowing
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
.
Namely we have
|I_{\rm diff}|+|I_{\rm nsf}|\leq 1
for all
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and the upper bound is sharp unless
A
is of type
{\rm E}_{8}
,
{\rm F}_{4}
or
{\rm G}_{2}
.
This extends the known result for involutive
\theta
that
\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
is reductive with abelian summand at most 1-dimensional, see
[
Ar62
]
.

Definition 3
.

The set of
weak Satake diagrams
is

(3.27)

\mathrm{WSat}(A)=\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash(\mathrm{Sat}(A)\cup\{\hbox to15.64pt{\vbox to4.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.18571pt\lower-2.48407pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 26.57 0 L 26.03 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 26.57 0 L 26.03 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.81252pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.65 4.87 C -3.35 3.04 0 0.3 0.91 0 C 0 -0.3 -3.35 -3.04 -3.65 -4.87}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 14.76 0 L 29.53 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}).

For
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
we will obtain a semidirect product decomposition in terms of a reductive Lie subalgebra and a nilpotent ideal.
For any
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and any
i\in I
denote by
\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r}
the span of all
b_{\bm{j}}
with
\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}
such that the coefficient of
\alpha_{i}
in
\alpha_{\bm{j}}
is at least
r
.
Set
\mathfrak{k}(i):=\mathfrak{k}(i)_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}
and recall the
\bm{\gamma}
-modified automorphism
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}
defined in (
2.20
).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
i
the unique element of
I\backslash X
such that
i=\tau(i)
and
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Then the following statements hold.

(i)

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
is
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}
-stable,
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}|_{\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}}
is an involution and
\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}:=\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
is its fixed-point subalgebra in
\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
;

(ii)

We have
{\rm ad}(b_{i})(\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r})\subseteq\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r+1}
for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and the subspaces
\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r}
are
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}})
-modules;

(iii)

The subspace
\mathfrak{k}(i)
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{k}
,
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}(i)\rtimes\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
and we have the lower central series

\mathfrak{k}(i)=\mathfrak{k}(i)_{1}\supset\mathfrak{k}(i)_{2}\supset\mathfrak{k}(i)_{3}=\mathfrak{k}(i)_{4}=\ldots=\{0\};

(iv)

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}}
is isomorphic to the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{X}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
,
b_{j;\gamma_{j}}
for
j\in I\backslash(X\cup\{i\})
and
b_{i;0}=f_{i}
.

Proof.

From the decomposition (
3.14
) it follows that
\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}=\langle\mathfrak{n}_{X}^{+},\mathfrak{h}^{\theta},\mathfrak{k}(i)_{0}\rangle
and, as vector spaces,
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}(i)\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
.
Crucially, by (
3.7
) we have
{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{M_{ij}}(b_{j})=0
for all
j\in I\backslash\{i\}
, since
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Now we prove the four statements consecutively.

(i)

Since
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})=\mathfrak{g}_{\theta(\alpha)}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
and applying
\theta=-w_{X}\tau
to any root of
\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
does not modify the coefficient of
\alpha_{i}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
is
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}
-stable.
Note that
\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
is fixed pointwise by
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}
.
Furthermore a dimension count in each simple summand of
\mathfrak{g}_{I\backslash\{i\}}
combined with (
3.22
) implies that
\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
is the fixed-point subalgebra of
\theta_{\bm{\gamma}}
.

(ii)

The first statement is immediate.
From the adjoint action of
e_{j}
(
j\in X
),
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
and
b_{j}
(
j\in I\backslash\{i\}
) on elements of
\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r}
, subject to
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
, we obtain that
{\rm ad}(\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}})(\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r})\subseteq\mathfrak{k}(i)_{r}
.

(iii)

From part (ii) it follows that
[\mathfrak{k}(i),\mathfrak{k}]\subset\mathfrak{k}(i)
and combining this with
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}(i)\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
we obtain the semidirect product decomposition.
A case-by-case analysis using Table
1
yields that the coefficient in front of
\alpha_{i}
in the highest root of
\Phi
is always 2.
This implies that the lower central series becomes trivial after 2 steps.

(iv)

This follows from the facts that the relations involving
b_{i;\gamma_{i}}
in
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
do not depend on
\gamma_{i}
and that the derivation of (
3.7
) did not require
\gamma_{i}\neq 0
.
∎

Example 2
.

We discuss two examples of
\mathfrak{k}(X,\tau)
with
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
.

(i)

The smallest such
\mathfrak{k}
occurs when
(X,\tau)=
1
2
.
By definition,
\mathfrak{k}
is the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{sp}_{4}
generated by
b_{1}=f_{1}+\gamma_{1}\theta(f_{1})
for some
\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
b_{2}=f_{2}
,
e_{2}
,
h_{2}
.
The relations
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
specialize to

(3.28)

\begin{gathered}=0,\qquad[e_{2},b_{2}]=h_{2},\qquad[h_{2},b_{1}]=b_{1},\qquad[h_{2},b_{2}]=-2b_{2},\\
[h_{2},e_{2}]=2e_{2},\qquad[b_{1},[b_{1},[b_{1},b_{2}]]]=0,\qquad[b_{2},[b_{2},b_{1}]]=0.\end{gathered}

According to (
3.21
), the standard basis of
\mathfrak{k}
is given by
\{e_{2},h_{2},b_{1},b_{2},b_{(1,2)},b_{(1,1,2)}\}
.
Proposition
3.2
implies
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
and Proposition
3.3
yields the nontrivial Levi decomposition
\mathfrak{k}=\mathrm{Sp}(b_{1},b_{(1,2)},b_{(1,1,2)})\rtimes\mathrm{Sp}(e_{2},h_{2},b_{2})
with the radical isomorphic to the 3-dimensional Heisenberg Lie algebra and the Levi subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.
In particular it follows from (
3.28
) that
b_{(1,1,2)}
is central.

(ii)

Proposition
3.3
excludes the case
(X,\tau)=

1
2

.
We will now see that is the only element of
\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
such that
\mathfrak{k}
is a reductive Lie algebra.
By definition,
\mathfrak{k}
is the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}={\rm Lie}(G_{2})
generated by
e_{1},h_{1},b_{1}=f_{1}
and
b_{2}=f_{2}+\gamma_{2}\,\theta(f_{2})
for some
\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
The relations
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
give

(3.29)

\begin{gathered}=h_{1},\qquad[e_{1},b_{2}]=0,\qquad[h_{1},b_{1}]=-2b_{1},\qquad[h_{1},b_{2}]=b_{2},\\
[h_{1},e_{1}]=2e_{1},\qquad[b_{1},[b_{1},b_{2}]]=0,\qquad[b_{2},[b_{2},[b_{2},[b_{2},b_{1}]]]]=-18\gamma_{2}^{2}e_{1}.\end{gathered}

The standard basis of
\mathfrak{k}
is given by
\{e_{1},h_{1},b_{1},b_{2},b_{(2,1)},b_{(2,2,1)},b_{(2,2,2,1)},b_{(1,2,2,2,1)}\}
.
Proposition
3.2
yields
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
using (
3.29
), the adjoint action of
e_{1}
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
on
\mathfrak{k}
implies that any ideal of
\mathfrak{k}
equals
\mathfrak{k}
if it contains any of the above standard basis elements.
Then some straightforward computations show that
\mathfrak{k}
is in fact a simple Lie algebra and hence isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
.

\diameter

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
.
Then
\mathfrak{k}
is not the fixed-point subalgebra of any
\phi\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
such that 1 is a simple root of the minimal polynomial of
\phi
.

Proof.

We first show this in the case
(X,\tau)=

1
2
.
Suppose there exists
\phi\in{\rm Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
such that
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\phi}
.
From
[h_{2},b_{1}]=3b_{1}
and
[h_{2},e_{1}]=-3e_{1}
one establishes straightforwardly that
\phi(h_{2})\in\mathfrak{h}
and hence that
\phi(h_{2})=\tfrac{3}{2}(m-1)h_{1}+mh_{2}
for some
m\in\mathbb{C}
.
Next, from
\theta(f_{2})\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}
it follows that
[h_{2},b_{2}]=-f_{2}-b_{2}
; hence
\phi(f_{2})=mf_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(1-m)b_{2}
.
Combining this with
[f_{2},b_{2}]\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}
one obtains
m=1
.
But this means that
h_{2}
and
f_{2}
are also fixed points of
\phi
, contrary to assumption.
Hence such
\phi
does not exist.

Now let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
.
Since
\mathfrak{k}
has a nonabelian nilpotent ideal by Proposition
3.3
,
\mathfrak{k}
is not a reductive Lie algebra.
Hence
[
Jac62
, Thm. 1]
implies the desired conclusion.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
3.4
,
\mathfrak{k}
is not the fixed-point subalgebra of any semisimple (in particular, finite-order) automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Finally we comment on the centre
\mathfrak{z}
of
\mathfrak{k}
for
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
.
In Example
2
(i) we saw that it is one-dimensional if
(X,\tau)=\hbox to15.64pt{\vbox to4.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-82.8742pt\lower-2.48407pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Let
