with
n>2
, in which case
\mathfrak{k}_{\hat{\imath}}
has a one-dimensional centre spanned by
b_{1}
.
Since
[b_{1},b_{2}]\neq 0
, it follows that also in this case
c^{\prime}=0
.
Hence
c\in\mathfrak{k}(i)
.
Since the centre of
\mathfrak{k}(i)
is
\mathfrak{k}(i)_{2}
we must have
\mathfrak{z}\subseteq\mathfrak{k}(i)_{2}
.
Define

\mathcal{J}_{\rm even}:=\{\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}\,\,|\,\,\text{the coefficient of }\alpha_{k}\text{ in }\alpha_{\bm{j}}\text{ is even for all }k\in I\backslash X\}.

Conjecture 3
.

If
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
, a single element of
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\bm{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{\rm even}}\mathbb{C}b_{\bm{j}}\subset\mathfrak{k}(i)_{2}
generates
\mathfrak{z}
.

Remark 4
.

This should be compared with the situation for Satake diagrams and the associated fixed-point subalgebras, where the centre of
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
has dimension
|I_{\rm diff}|+|I_{\rm nsf}|\in\{0,1\}
.
Casework suggests that it is generated by a linear combination of either
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}
(for
i\in I_{\rm diff}
) or
b_{i}
(for
i\in I_{\rm nsf}
) and at least one other standard basis element of
\mathfrak{k}
.

\diameter

3.3.
The universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{k})

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
.
We identify
\mathfrak{k}
with its image in
U(\mathfrak{k})
under the canonical Lie algebra embedding.
The generators of
U(\mathfrak{k})
corresponding to
b_{i}

(i\in I\backslash X)
can be modified by scalar terms, which is a straightforward generalization of
[
Ko14
, Cor. 2.9]
.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
, the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}})_{\bm{\sigma}}
is generated by
e_{i},f_{i}
(
i\in X
),
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
and

(3.30)

b_{i;\gamma_{i},\sigma_{i}}=f_{i}+\gamma_{i}\,\theta(f_{i})+\sigma_{i}\qquad\text{for all }i\in I\backslash X.

Again, if there is no cause for confusion, we will suppress
\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\sigma}
from the notation.
From Conjecture
2
we obtain the following conditional result for
U(\mathfrak{k})
, which would address the question raised in
[
Ko14
, Rmk. 2.10]
: for
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
,
U(\mathfrak{k})
is isomorphic to the algebra with generators
h_{i},e_{i}
(
i\in X
),
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}
(
i\in I^{*},i\neq\tau(i)
),
b_{i}
(
i\in I
) and relations
(
3.2
-
3.7
)
.

We may view
U(\mathfrak{k})
as a Hopf subalgebra of
U(\mathfrak{g})
so that Lie algebra automorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
lift to Hopf algebra automorphisms of
U(\mathfrak{g})
.
Call two Hopf subalgebras
B,B^{\prime}
of
U(\mathfrak{g})

equivalent
if there exists
\phi\in{\rm Aut}_{\rm Hopf}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
such that
B^{\prime}=\phi(B)
.
Define

(3.31)

\widetilde{\Gamma}:=\{\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\gamma_{i}=1\text{ if }i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm diff}\},\qquad\Sigma:=\{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}\,\,|\,\,\sigma_{i}=0\text{ if }i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm nsf}\}.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
.
There exist
\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}\in\widetilde{\Gamma}
and
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\Sigma
such that
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}})_{\bm{\sigma}}
is equivalent to
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}})_{\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The existence of
\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}
follows from an argument analogous to the proof of
[
Ko14
, Prop. 9.2 (i)]
.
Hence
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\gamma}})_{\bm{\sigma}}
is equivalent to
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}})_{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}
for some
\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
.
Regarding the existence of
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, note that
b_{i;\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}}\in(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}})^{\prime}
if
i\notin I_{\rm nsf}
, by Proposition
3.2
.
Hence
U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bm{\widetilde{\gamma}}})_{\bm{\widetilde{\sigma}}}
is already generated by
e_{i},f_{i}
(
i\in X
),
h\in\mathfrak{h}^{\theta}
,
b_{i;\widetilde{\gamma}_{i},0}
for
i\in(I\backslash X)\backslash I_{\rm nsf}
and
b_{i;\widetilde{\gamma}_{i},\widetilde{s}_{i}}
for
i\in I_{\rm nsf}
.
Hence we may take
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}
if
i\in I_{\rm nsf}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}=0
otherwise.
∎

4.
Quantum pair algebras and the universal K-matrix revisited

Assume the
d_{i}
are dyadic rationals and let
\mathbb{K}
be a quadratic closure of
\mathbb{C}(q)
where
q
is an indeterminate, so that
q_{i}:=q^{d_{i}}\in\mathbb{K}
for all
i\in I
.
The Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is an associative unital algebra over
\mathbb{K}
which quantizes the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
.
It is generated by
\{E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}\}
where
i\in I
, satisfying the relations given in e.g.
[
Lu94
, 3.1.1]
.
The Hopf algebra structure is the one defined in
[
Lu94
, 3.1.3, 3.1.11, 3.3]
.
We write
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
for the Hopf subalgebra generated by
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
for
i\in I
.
We also write
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{\pm})
for the coideal subalgebras generated by the
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
(
i\in I
), respectively.
The algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is
Q
-graded in terms of the root spaces
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{\alpha}=\{u\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\,|\,K_{i}uK_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{\alpha(h_{i})}u\text{ for all }i\in I\}
.

We discuss some automorphisms of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.
Diagram automorphisms act (by relabelling) as Hopf algebra automorphisms on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.
Moreover, we have Lusztig’s automorphisms
T_{i}
for
i\in I
, given as
T^{\prime\prime}_{i,1}
in
[
Lu94
, 37.1.3]
, which generate an image of the braid group in
{\rm Aut}_{\rm alg}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
and reproduce
{\rm Ad}(s_{i})
as
q\to 1
.
They satisfy
T_{i}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{\alpha})\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{s_{i}(\alpha)}
for all
\alpha\in Q
and
T_{i}(K_{j})=K_{j}K_{i}^{-a_{ij}}
for all
j\in I
.
For
X\subseteq I
with
w_{X}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
a reduced decomposition we write
T_{w_{X}}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{\ell}}
.
If
\tau\in{\rm Aut}(A)
stabilizes
X
then
[\tau,T_{w_{X}}]=0
.
Finally, the assignments

(4.1)

\omega_{q}(E_{i})=-K_{i}^{-1}F_{i},\qquad\omega_{q}(F_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i},\qquad\omega_{q}(K_{i}^{\pm 1})=K_{i}^{\mp 1}\qquad\qquad\text{for }i\in I

define
\omega_{q}\in{\rm Aut}_{\rm alg}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}))
which is a particular quantum analogue of the Chevalley involution commuting with each
T_{i}
, see
[
BK19
, Lemma 7.1]
, and with
{\rm Aut}(A)
.

We now discuss the changes to definitions and statements in the papers
[
Ko14
,
BK15
,
BK19
,
Ko20
,
DK19
]
needed to generalize these works to all generalized Satake diagrams.

In the remainder of this section we assume
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
.
The quantum analogon of the map
\theta={\rm Ad}(w_{X})\tau\omega
is the map

(4.2)

\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}(X,\tau)=T_{w_{X}}\tau\,\omega_{q}\in{\rm Aut}_{\rm alg}(U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})).

The quantization of the fixed-point subalgebra in the formalism by
[
Ko14
]
relies on the description of
\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}
in terms of generators given in
[
Ko14
, Lemma 2.8]
.
Our
\mathfrak{k}(X,\tau)
by definition can be quantized to a right coideal subalgebra in the same way.

Definition 4
.

Let
\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{I\backslash X}
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{K}^{I\backslash X}
.
The
quantum pair algebra

B=B_{\bm{\gamma},\bm{\sigma}}(X,\tau)
is the coideal subalgebra generated by
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{X})
,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})
and the elements

(4.3)

B_{i}=B_{i;\bm{\gamma},\bm{\sigma}}=F_{i}+\gamma_{i}\theta_{q}(F_{i}K_{i})K_{i}^{-1}+\sigma_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\qquad\text{for all }i\in I\backslash X.

Remark 5
.

(i)

Note that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})
is denoted
{U^{0}_{\Theta}}^{\prime}
in
[
Ko14
]
.
In
[
Le03
, Eq. (2.4)]
and
[
Ko14
, Def. 5.1]

\sigma_{i}
is denoted
s_{i}
(we use a different notation to avoid confusion with the simple reflections
s_{i}
and the group homomorphism
s\in\widetilde{H}
).
The scalar
\gamma_{i}
corresponds to the scalar
d_{i}
in
[
Le03
, Eq. (2.4)]
and the scalar
c_{i}
in
[
Ko14
, Def. 5.1]
.
More precisely, the Kolb-Balagović formalism fits in our approach upon setting

(4.4)

\gamma_{i}=s(\alpha_{\tau(i)})\,c_{i}\qquad\text{for all }i\in I\backslash X,

also see
[
BK19
, Eq. (7.7)]
.

(ii)

Comparing with
[
Ko14
, Def. 4.3]
or
[
BK19
, Def. 5.4 and Eq. (5.4)]
, note the absence of the factor
{\rm Ad}(s)
from our definition of
\theta_{q}
.
Here
s\in\widetilde{H}
is required to satisfy
[
BK19
, Eqs. (5.1)-(5.2)]
so that
\theta_{q}
specializes to the appropriate Lie algebra involution in the case
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
, see
[
Ko14
, Prop. 10.2]
.
In our case this is unnecessary; to compare with these earlier papers there are in fact two natural choices for
s
, see
[
BK19
, Rmk. 5.2]
, one of which is satisfied for instance by
s=\chi_{(1,1,\ldots,1)}
(see (
2.20
) for the notation) which takes values in
\{\pm 1\}
.
\diameter

Moreover, if
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
and the tuples
\bm{\gamma}
,
\bm{\sigma}
lie in the sets

(4.5)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{q}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{\gamma}\in(\mathbb{K}^{\times})^{I\backslash X}\,\,|\,\,\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{\tau(i)}\text{ if }i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm diff}\},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{q}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathbb{K}^{I\backslash X}\,\,|\,\,\,\sigma_{i}=0\text{ if }i\in I^{*}\backslash I_{\rm nsf}\},

respectively, then in
[
Ko14
, Sec. 5.3 and Sec. 6]
decompositions of
B
are obtained which culminate in the quantum analogue of (
3.15
), namely
B\cap U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})=U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})
.
The key condition for Satake diagrams, see (
2.19
), is only used in
[
Ko14
, Proof of Lemma 5.11, Step 1]
, but it is clear that what is needed there is precisely the weaker condition appearing in the definition of a generalized Satake diagram, see Definition
1
.
Furthermore, in
[
Ko14
, Thms. 7.4 and 7.8]
quantum Serre relations for the
B_{i}
are found for low values of
-a_{ij}
and the results were extended in
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.7, Case 4]
; we discuss the generalization to
\mathrm{GSat}(A)
in Section
4.2
.
The rest of
[
Ko14
]
is applicable without change in the setting of generalized Satake diagrams; in particular in the specialization
q\to 1
one recovers
U(\mathfrak{k})
, see
[
Ko14
, Sec. 10]
.

4.1.
The bar involution for the subalgebra
B

The bar involution
\overline{\phantom{x}}
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is the algebra automorphism of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
fixing
E_{i},F_{i}
and inverting
K_{i}^{\pm 1}
and
q
; it plays a crucial role in Lusztig’s construction of the quasi R-matrix, see
[
Lu94
]
.
In order to have a natural modification of Lusztig’s theory of bar involutions and quasi R-matrices to the setting of quantum symmetric pair algebras, the paper
[
BK15
]
deals with the existence of an analogous map of
B
.
This follows earlier work by
[
ES13
]
and
[
BW18a
]
in the case of certain quantum symmetric pairs of type AIII.
More precisely, for suitable parameters
\bm{\gamma}
, there exists an involutive algebra automorphism
\overline{\phantom{x}}^{B}:B\to B
which coincides with
\overline{\phantom{x}}
on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{X})U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})
and satisfies
\overline{B_{i}}^{B}=B_{i}
for
i\in I\backslash X
, see
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.11, Cor. 3.13, Rmk. 3.15]
.
The defining condition of Satake diagrams is not used explicitly in
[
BK15
]
but casework is used in the results
[
BK15
, Props. 2.3 and 3.8]
which needs to be extended to the new diagrams in Table
1
, which we will now explain.
We also note that the result
[
BK15
, Prop. 2.3]
was generalized in
[
BW18c
, Thm. 4.1]
to the Kac-Moody setting, but this did not explicitly include the cases where
B
is defined in terms of
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)\backslash\mathrm{Sat}(A)
.
Here we provide an elementary proof for
\mathfrak{g}
of finite type which works for all compatible decorations.

Let
\sigma
be the unique algebra anti-automorphism of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
which fixes
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
and inverts
K_{i}
.
For
i\in I
, denote by
r_{i}
Lusztig’s
right skew derivation
, see
[
Lu94
, 1.2.13]
; it is the unique linear map on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
such that for all
x,y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
with
y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{\mu}
(
\mu\in Q^{+}
) we have

(4.6)

r_{i}(xy)=q_{i}^{\mu(h_{i})}r_{i}(x)y+xr_{i}(y).

We denote
[x,y]_{p}:=xy-pyx
for
x,y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
p\in\mathbb{K}
; note that
\sigma([x,y]_{p})=[\sigma(y),\sigma(x)]_{p}
.
The definition of
T_{j}
implies that
T_{j}(E_{i})=E_{i}
if
a_{ji}=0
and
T_{j}(E_{i})=[E_{j},E_{i}]_{q_{j}^{-1}}
if
a_{ji}=-1
.
We start with a lemma that simplifies the proof drastically.
Call a connected component of
X

simple
if it is of the form
\{j\}
for some
j\in I
such that
a_{ij}=a_{ji}\in\{0,-1\}
for all
i\in I\backslash X
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
i\in I\backslash X
and suppose
\emptyset\neq X(i)=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{\ell}
is a decomposition into connected components.
If
i\neq\tau(i)
then
\ell\leq 1
and if
i=\tau(i)
, all
X_{s}
are simple except at most one.
Denote by
Y
the non-simple connected component of
X(i)
if present and otherwise let
Y
be any simple connected component.
If
(r_{i}T_{w_{Y}})(E_{i})
is fixed by
\sigma\tau
then so is
(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})
.

Proof.

The first part of the Lemma follows from the classification of Satake diagrams in
[
Ar62
]
and an inspection of Table
1
.
Since adding simple components does not change the statement, it is true for all compatible decorations.

The second part is proven by induction with respect to the number of simple components.
If there are none, then
X(i)=Y
and the statement is true.
Otherwise, by the induction hypothesis we may suppose
X(i)=X^{\prime}\cup\{j\}
where
(\sigma\tau)(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i}))=(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i})
,
\{j\}
is a simple component of
X
and
a_{jk}=0
for all
k\in X^{\prime}
.
Hence
T_{X(i)}=T_{w_{X^{\prime}}}T_{j}
so that
T_{w_{X}}(E_{i})=T_{X(i)}(E_{i})=T_{w_{X^{\prime}}}([E_{j},E_{i}]_{q_{j}^{-1}})=[E_{j},T_{w_{X^{\prime}}}(E_{i})]_{q_{j}^{-1}}
.
By (
4.6
) we have
(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})=[E_{j},(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i})]_{q_{j}^{-2}}
.
Since
(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i})
lies in
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}_{X^{\prime}})
it commutes with
E_{j}
.
Hence

(4.7)

(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})=(1-q_{j}^{-2})E_{j}(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i})=(1-q_{j}^{-2})(r_{i}T_{w_{X^{\prime}}})(E_{i})E_{j}.

Since
\tau(j)=j
, applying
\sigma\tau
yields the desired result.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

For all
i\in I\backslash X
,
(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})
is fixed by
\sigma\tau
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially casework, but first we make some observations.

(i)

Since
T_{w_{X}}(E_{i})=T_{X(i)}(E_{i})
we may assume that
\{i,\tau(i)\}
is the only
\tau
-orbit outside
X
.

(ii)

We may assume
X
is nonempty as otherwise
(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})=1
.

(iii)

By Lemma
4.1
it suffices to prove the statement in the case that
X
is connected.

(iv)

If
|X|=1
, we write
X=\{j\}
with
\tau(j)=j
.
Then
T_{w_{X}}(E_{i})=T_{j}(E_{i})\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{s_{j}(\alpha_{i})}\cap U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
.
Hence
(r_{i}T_{w_{X}})(E_{i})\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{s_{j}(\alpha_{i})-\alpha_{i}}\cap U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})=\mathbb{K}E_{j}^{-a_{ji}}
so it is fixed by
\sigma\tau
.

(v)

In
[
BK15
, Proof of Prop. 2.3]
the statement was proved for all Satake diagrams.

Hence it suffices to prove the statement for those diagrams in Table
1
where the node
i
is the only node outside
X
,
X
is connected and
|X|>1
.
The only infinite family satisfying this condition is given by

.
In this case the proof is identical to the proof for the type BII case in
[
BK15
, Prop. 2.3]
.
The exceptional diagrams satisfying this condition are

We give here the proof for the last case which is very much in the spirit of
[
BK15
, Proof of Prop. 2.3]
; the proofs for the other three cases are similar and are left to the reader.

We label the nodes as
1
2
3
4

and assume
d_{1}=d_{2}=2
and
d_{3}=d_{4}=1
for convenience.
Note that
\tau=\mathrm{id}
.
The reduced decompositions
w_{X}=(s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{4}s_{3}s_{2})(s_{4}s_{3}s_{4})=(s_{4}s_{3}s_{4})(s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{4}s_{3}s_{2})
yield

(4.8)

T_{w_{X}}(E_{1})=(T_{2}T_{3}T_{2}T_{4}T_{3}T_{2})(E_{1})=(T_{4}T_{3}T_{4}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1}).

From the first expression we readily obtain

(4.9)

T_{w_{X}}(E_{1})=[(T_{2}T_{3}T_{2}T_{4}T_{3})(E_{2}),[(T_{2}T_{3})(E_{2}),[E_{2},E_{1}]_{q^{-2}}]_{q^{-2}}]_{q^{-2}}.

Now note that
(s_{3}s_{4}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{4}s_{3})(\alpha_{2})=(s_{3}s_{2}s_{3})(\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{2}
and
s_{3}s_{4}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}s_{4}s_{3}
and
s_{3}s_{2}s_{3}
are reduced elements in
W
.
Appealing to
[
Jan96
, Prop. 8.20]
we arrive at

(4.10)

T_{w_{X}}(E_{1})=[(T_{4}^{-1}T_{3}^{-1})(E_{2}),[T_{3}^{-1}(E_{2}),[E_{2},E_{1}]_{q^{-2}}]_{q^{-2}}]_{q^{-2}}

so that (
4.6
) implies

(4.11)

(r_{1}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1})=(1-q^{-4})[(T_{4}^{-1}T_{3}^{-1})(E_{2}),[T_{3}^{-1}(E_{2}),E_{2}]_{q^{-4}}]_{q^{-4}}.

Applying
\sigma\tau
and using
T_{i}\sigma=\sigma T_{i}^{-1}
(see e.g.
[
Lu94
, 37.2.4]
) we obtain

(4.12)

\displaystyle(\sigma\tau)((r_{1}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1}))=(1-q^{-4})[[E_{2},T_{3}(E_{2})]_{q^{-4}},(T_{4}T_{3})(E_{2})]_{q^{-4}}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-4})\left(q^{-4}[T_{3}(E_{2}),T_{4}([T_{3}(E_{2}),E_{2}])]+[E_{2},[T_{3}(E_{2}),(T_{4}T_{3})(E_{2})]_{q^{-4}}]_{q^{-4}}\right).

By the q-Serre relation
E_{2}^{2}E_{3}-(q^{2}+q^{-2})E_{2}E_{3}E_{2}+E_{3}E_{2}^{2}=0
we have

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q+q^{-1}}[E_{3}^{2}E_{2}-(1+q^{-2})E_{3}E_{2}E_{3}+q^{-2}E_{2}E_{3}^{2},E_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}-1}{q+q^{-1}}\left(E_{3}E_{2}E_{3}E_{2}-q^{-2}E_{3}E_{2}^{2}E_{3}-q^{-2}E_{2}E_{3}^{3}E_{2}+q^{-4}E_{2}E_{3}E_{2}E_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=(q^{2}-1)[E_{3},E_{2}]_{q^{-2}}^{2}=(q^{2}-1)\sigma\big([E_{2},E_{3}]_{q^{-2}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=(q^{2}-1)(\sigma T_{2})(E_{3}^{2})\hskip 2.84526pt=(q^{2}-1)T_{2}^{-1}(E_{3}^{2})

so that for the first term of (
4.12
) we have

(4.14)

[T_{3}(E_{2}),T_{4}([T_{3}(E_{2}),E_{2}])]=(q^{2}-1)T_{2}^{-1}\left([(T_{2}T_{3})(E_{2}),T_{4}(E_{3}^{2})]\right).

The reduced elements
s_{3}s_{2}s_{3}
and
s_{3}s_{4}
map
\alpha_{2}
to itself and
\alpha_{3}
to
\alpha_{4}
, respectively, so that
(T_{2}T_{3})(E_{2})=T_{3}^{-1}(E_{2})
and
T_{4}(E_{3})=T_{3}^{-1}(E_{4})
by
[
Jan96
, Prop. 8.20]
.
Hence

(4.15)

[T_{3}(E_{2}),T_{4}([T_{3}(E_{2}),E_{2}])]=(q^{2}-1)(T_{2}^{-1}T_{3}^{-1})\left([E_{2},E_{4}^{2}]\right)=0

where we have used the q-Serre relation
E_{2}E_{4}-E_{4}E_{2}=0
.
As a consequence, (
4.12
) yields

(4.16)

\displaystyle(\sigma\tau)((r_{1}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1}))

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-4})[E_{2},[T_{3}(E_{2}),(T_{4}T_{3})(E_{2})]_{q^{-4}}]_{q^{-4}}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-4})(T_{4}T_{3}T_{4})\left([(T_{4}^{-1}T_{3}^{-1})(E_{2}),[T_{3}^{-1}(E_{2}),E_{2}]_{q^{-4}}]_{q^{-4}}\right)

\displaystyle=(T_{4}T_{3}T_{4}r_{1}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1})

where we have used
T_{3}T_{4}T_{3}=T_{4}T_{3}T_{4}
.
Because
r_{i}
and
T_{j}
commute if
a_{ij}=0
we have
(\sigma\tau)((r_{1}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1}))=(r_{1}T_{4}T_{3}T_{4}T_{w_{X}})(E_{1})
and by virtue of (
4.8
) the proof is complete.
∎

4.2.
Quantum Serre relations for the
B_{i}

We now introduce some notation in order to discuss q-Serre relations for the
B_{i}
.
For
x,y\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
recall the shorthand
M_{ij}=1-a_{ij}
and define

(4.17)

F_{ij}(x,y)=\sum_{n=0}^{M_{ij}}(-1)^{n}{M_{ij}\brack n}_{q_{i}}x^{M_{ij}-n}yx^{n}.

Here
{M_{ij}\brack n}_{q_{i}}
is the
q_{i}
-deformed binomial coefficient defined in
[
Lu94
, 1.3.3]
.
Note that the q-Serre relations for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
are the relations
F_{ij}(E_{i},E_{j})=F_{ij}(F_{i},F_{j})=0
for all
i,j\in I
with
i\neq j
.
We denote
B_{i}=F_{i}
if
i\in X
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X})=\langle E_{i}\,|\,i\in X\rangle
.
For
i\in I\backslash X
we write

(4.18)

Y_{i}=(r_{\tau(i)}\theta_{q})(F_{i}K_{i})K_{i}^{-1}K_{\tau(i)}=-(r_{\tau(i)}T_{w_{X}})(E_{\tau(i)})K_{i}^{-1}K_{\tau(i)}

and we write
[n]_{q_{i}}=\frac{q_{i}^{n}-q_{i}^{-n}}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i\in I
.

According to
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.7 (Case 4) and Thm. 3.9]
, for all
i,j\in I
such that
i\neq j
the elements
F_{ij}(B_{i},B_{j})
are
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X})U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}^{\theta})
-linear combinations of products
B_{i_{1}}\cdots B_{i_{\ell}}
with
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}\in I
satisfying
\alpha_{i_{1}}+\ldots+\alpha_{i_{\ell}}<\lambda_{ij}
.
Moreover, these expressions vanish if
\tau(i)\notin\{i,j\}
as a consequence of
[
Ko14
, Thm. 7.3]
and if
i\in X
, see
[
Ko14
, Eq. (5.20)]
.
If
\tau(i)=j\in I\backslash X
they are given by
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.6]
for all values of
a_{ij}
.
Now suppose
\tau(i)=i\in I\backslash X
.
Then the expressions are given in
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.7]
if
j\in I\backslash(X\cup\{i\})
and
a_{ij}\in\{0,-1,-2,-3\}
and in
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.8]
if
j\in X
and
a_{ij}\in\{0,-1,-2\}
.

These theorems cover all generalized Satake diagrams in Table
1
except
1
2

which we discuss now; without loss of generality we may assume
d_{1}=3
and
d_{2}=1
.
Note that
Y_{2}=-r_{2}(T_{1}(E_{2}))=-q^{-3}(q^{3}-q^{-3})E_{1}
.
In this case we have, by a direct computation,

(4.19)

\displaystyle F_{21}(B_{2},B_{1})

\displaystyle=\left(([3]_{q}^{2}+1)(B_{2}^{2}B_{1}+B_{1}B_{2}^{2})-[4]_{q}([2]_{q}^{2}+1)B_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\right)q\gamma_{2}Y_{2}+

\displaystyle-[3]_{q}^{2}B_{1}(q\gamma_{2}Y_{2})^{2}+[2]_{q}B_{2}^{2}\big(r_{1}(q\gamma_{2}Y_{2})q^{6}K_{1}+(\sigma r_{1}\sigma)(q\gamma_{2}Y_{2})q^{-6}K_{1}^{-1}\big)+

\displaystyle-\frac{[3]_{q}}{(q-q^{-1})(q^{3}-q^{-3})}q\gamma_{2}Y_{2}\big(r_{1}(q\gamma_{2}Y_{2})q^{9}K_{1}+(\sigma r_{1}\sigma)(q\gamma_{2}Y_{2})q^{-9}K_{1}^{-1}\big).

Remark 6
.

In the formula
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.7,Eq. (3.21)]
for
a_{ij}=-3
, which deals with the case
j\in I\backslash X
, the sign of the terms quadratic in
B_{i}
is incorrect.
This does not affect the later result
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.11]
.
Up to this sign issue, the first terms of (
4.19
) directly match
[
BK15
, Eq. (3.21)]
, so that also in the case
a_{ij}=-3
we expect a unified expression in the style of
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.9]
in the general Kac-Moody setting.
\diameter

We now review the arguments from
[
BK15
]
in terms of
\gamma_{i}
and
Y_{i}
to show that the bar involution exists for general
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
with
A
of finite type.
First of all, note that the proof of
[
BK15
, Prop. 3.5]
does not require the specific choice of
s\in\widetilde{H}
given in
[
BK15
, Eq. (3.2)]
but any
s
satisfying the weaker constraint
[
BK19
, Eqs. (5.1-5.2)]
, also see
[
BK19
, Rmk. 5.2]
.
With that qualification, the arguments of the proof of
[
BK15
, Prop. 3.5]
imply that

(4.20)

\overline{Y_{i}}=q_{i}^{(\alpha_{i}-w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-2\rho_{X})(h_{i})}\zeta(\alpha_{i})Y_{\tau(i)}

where
\rho_{X}\in(\mathfrak{h}_{X})^{*}
is the Weyl vector of
\mathfrak{g}_{X}
; this specializes to
[
BK15
, Prop. 3.5]
if we set
\gamma_{i}=s(\alpha_{\tau(i)})c_{i}
.
Note that Proposition
4.2
is crucial in this context; in the notation of
[
BK15
,
BK19
]
, we have
\nu_{i}=1
for all
i\in I\backslash X
, for all
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
of finite type.
The proof of
[
BK15
, Thm. 3.11]
, which requires (
4.19
) in the special case
, implies that the existence of the bar involution for
B
is equivalent to

(4.21)

\overline{\gamma_{i}Y_{i}}=q_{i}^{\alpha_{\tau(i)}(h_{i})}\gamma_{\tau(i)}Y_{\tau(i)}

for all
i\in I\backslash X
, which specializes to
[
BK15
, Eq. (3.28)]
if
\gamma_{i}=s(\alpha_{\tau(i)})c_{i}
.
Combining
(
4.20
-
4.21
)
, the existence of the bar involution for
B
is equivalent to

(4.22)

\gamma_{\tau(i)}=\zeta(\alpha_{i})q_{i}^{(\theta(\alpha_{i})-2\rho_{X})(h_{i})}\overline{\gamma_{i}}

where we have used the analogue of (
2.13
) for roots, which can be obtained in the same way.

Remark 7
.

Suppose
i\in I\backslash X
is such that
\tau(i)=i
and
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Then (
4.22
) simplifies to
\gamma_{i}=-q_{i}^{(\theta(\alpha_{i})-2\rho_{X})(h_{i})}\overline{\gamma_{i}}
so that
\gamma_{i}\to 0
as
q\to 1
.
For
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{WSat}(A)
this is compatible with Proposition
3.3
(iv).
\diameter

4.3.
The universal K-matrix

Building upon the work in
[
BK15
]
on the bar involution, in
[
BK19
]
the universal K-matrix
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(X,\tau)
for
B=B(X,\tau)
is constructed for
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
and the relations (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) are derived.
Again we comment on those places in this text with a nontrivial generalization to the setting of quantum pair algebras defined in terms of generalized Satake diagrams.
In addition to (
4.22
) we also assume

(4.23)

\overline{\sigma_{i}}=\sigma_{i},

see
[
BK19
, Eq. (5.16)]
.
In
[
BK19
, Proof of Lemma 6.4]
the defining condition of Satake diagrams is used, but only the defining condition of generalized Satake diagrams is needed.
In
[
BK19
, Rmk. 7.2]
a case-by-case analysis is employed based on the list of Satake diagrams in
[
Ar62
]
.
Denoting by
\tau_{0}
the diagram automorphism corresponding to the longest element of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
, it is derived that
\tau_{0}
preserves
X
and commutes with
\tau
; furthermore one can choose
\bm{\gamma}\in\Gamma
and
\bm{\sigma}\in\Sigma
such that
\gamma_{\tau(i)}=\gamma_{\tau_{0}(i)}
and
\sigma_{\tau(i)}=\sigma_{\tau_{0}(i)}
for all
i\in I\backslash X
and the above transformation properties with respect to the bar involution hold.
Extending this analysis to Table
1
, one checks that the remark is valid for all generalized Satake diagrams.

In
[
Ko20
]
it is shown that
\mathcal{K}
satisfies the axiom (
1.2
) for a universal K-matrix and the centre of
B
is described in terms of
\mathcal{K}
, without using the defining condition of Satake diagrams or a case-by-case analysis; it follows that the results remain valid for
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{GSat}(A)
.

This is also the case for
[
DK19
, Section 2]
which entails an analysis of the restricted Weyl group and restricted root system following
[
Lu76
]
in order to establish a factorization of the quasi K-matrix (subject to a condition on Satake diagrams of restricted rank 2).
In particular, from the statement
\tau_{0}(X)=X
for any
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{Sat}(A)
it is inferred that
\tau_{0,X[i]}(X)=X
for all
i\in I^{*}
and hence
\{w_{X}w_{X[i]}\,|\,i\in I^{*}\}
is a Coxeter system for the group it generates.
For all generalized Satake diagrams these conclusions follow directly from Theorem
2.1
.

Appendix A
Deriving Serre relations for
\mathfrak{k}

The following three technical lemmas are used to derive the key equation (
3.7
).
It is convenient to introduce the notation
Q^{+}_{X}:=Q^{+}\cap Q_{X}=\sum_{i\in X}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Fix
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
,
i\in X
and
j\in I
.
For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have

(A.1)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m}(b_{j})=\begin{cases}{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})+\gamma_{j}\,\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})\right)&\text{if }j\in I\backslash X,\\
{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})&\text{if }j\in X.\end{cases}

Proof.

Because
\theta
is a Lie algebra automorphism this follows immediately from (
2.12
)
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Fix
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
,
i\in I\backslash X
and
j\in X
.
For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have

(A.2)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m}(b_{j})={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{m}\,\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})\right)+L_{m}

where

(A.3)

L_{m}=\begin{cases}(1+\zeta(\alpha_{i}))\,\gamma_{i}\,[\theta(f_{i}),[f_{i},f_{j}]]\in\mathfrak{n}_{X}^{+}&\text{if }\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{+},\,m=2,\\
-\gamma_{i}\,(2h_{i}-a_{ij}h_{j})&\text{if }\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j},\,m=2,\\
-3\,(2+a_{ij})\,\gamma_{i}\,(f_{i}-\theta(f_{i}))&\text{if }\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j},\,m=3,\\
-6\,a_{ij}(2+a_{ij})\,\gamma_{i}^{2}\,e_{j}&\text{if }\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j},\,m=4,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\hskip-5.69054pt

Proof.

By induction with respect to
m
.
For
m=1
, (
2.12
) implies

(A.4)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{1}(b_{j})=[f_{i}+\gamma_{i}\,\theta(f_{i}),f_{j}]={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{1}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{1}\,\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})(f_{j})\right)+L_{1}

with
L_{1}=0
as required.
Now assume
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
and suppose the statement holds for all smaller values.
Then, by virtue of the induction hypothesis, the fact that
\theta
is a Lie algebra automorphism and (
2.12
), we find

(A.5)

\displaystyle{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m}(b_{j})

\displaystyle=\left[b_{i},{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m-1}(b_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\left[f_{i}+\gamma_{i}\,\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right)+L_{m-1}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{m}\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})\right)

\displaystyle+\gamma_{i}\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]+\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\left[f_{i},\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right)\right]+\left[b_{i},L_{m-1}\right].\hskip-5.69054pt

Using (
2.12
) we have
\theta^{2}(f_{i})=\zeta(\alpha_{i})f_{i}
so that

(A.6)

L_{m}=\gamma_{i}\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]+\zeta(\alpha_{i})\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\theta\left(\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]\right)+[b_{i},L_{m-1}].

Suppose that
\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]\neq 0
.
Then
w_{X}(\alpha_{\tau(i)})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi\cup\{0\}
.
Now
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi^{-}
implies that
\tau(i)=i
and
j\in X(i)
.

First we consider the case
w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{+}
.
Then
m=2
and since
w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in Q^{+}_{X}
it follows that
[\theta(f_{i}),[f_{i},f_{j}]]\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}
.
The claimed expression for
L_{2}
follows immediately from (
A.6
) and those for
L_{m}
with
m>2
from (
3.2
).

If
w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\cup\{0\}
then
w_{X}(\alpha_{i})\leq(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}
.
Hence
X(i)=\{j\}
and we obtain

(A.7)

w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}=(2-m)\,\alpha_{i}-(1+a_{ji})\,\alpha_{j}.

From
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi^{-}
it follows that
a_{ji}=-1
.
Now
\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\cap\Phi=\{\pm\alpha_{i}\}
implies that
m\in\{2,3\}
.
Straightforwardly we compute

(A.8)

\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})(f_{j})\right]=a_{ij}h_{j}-h_{i},\qquad\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{2}(f_{j})\right]=-2(1+a_{ij})f_{i},

from which the claimed expressions for
L_{m}
readily follow.
∎

Recall the integer
p_{ij}^{(r,m)}
defined by (
3.8
).

Lemma A.3
.

Fix
(X,\tau)\in\mathrm{CDec}(A)
,
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{I\backslash X}
and
i,j\in I\backslash X
such that
i\neq j
.
Recall the integer
p_{ij}^{(r,m)}
defined by (
3.8
).
For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

(A.9)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m}(b_{j})={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{m}\gamma_{j}\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m}(f_{j})\right)+L_{m}

where

(A.10)

L_{m}=\begin{cases}\left(\gamma_{i}+\zeta(\alpha_{i})\gamma_{j}\right)[\theta(f_{i}),f_{j}]\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{X}&\text{if }\tau(i)=j,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})-\alpha_{i}\in\Phi^{+},\,m=1,\\
\gamma_{j}h_{i}-\gamma_{i}h_{j}&\text{if }\tau(i)=j,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i},\,m=1,\\
2\left((\gamma_{j}-a_{ij}\gamma_{i})f_{i}-\gamma_{i}(\gamma_{i}-a_{ij}\gamma_{j})e_{j}\right)&\text{if }\tau(i)=j,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i},\,m=2,\\
\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}p_{ij}^{(r,m)}\gamma_{i}^{r}{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m-2r}(b_{j})&\text{if }\tau(i)=i,\,w_{X}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

As before we apply induction with respect to
m
.
For
m=0
we have

(A.11)

{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{0}(b_{j})=b_{j}=f_{j}+\gamma_{j}\,\theta(f_{j})={\rm ad}(f_{i})^{0}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{0}\gamma_{j}\,\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{0}(f_{j})\right)+L_{0}

with
L_{0}=0
as required.
Now assume
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and suppose the statement holds for all smaller values.
Then, by the induction hypothesis,

(A.12)

\displaystyle{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m}(b_{j})

\displaystyle=[b_{i},{\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m-1}(b_{j})]

\displaystyle=[f_{i}+\gamma_{i}\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})+\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\gamma_{j}\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right)+L_{m-1}].

Since
\theta
is a Lie algebra automorphism, rearranging terms yields (
A.9
) with
L_{m}\in\mathfrak{g}
defined by

(A.13)

L_{m}=\gamma_{i}\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]+\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\gamma_{j}\left[f_{i},\theta\left({\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right)\right]+[b_{i},L_{m-1}].

Using (
2.12
) we obtain

(A.14)

L_{m}=\gamma_{i}\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]+\zeta(\alpha_{i})\gamma_{i}^{m-1}\gamma_{j}\,\theta\left(\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]\right)+[b_{i},L_{m-1}].

If
\left[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})\right]\neq 0
then
w_{X}(\alpha_{\tau(i)})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi\cup\{0\}
.

If
w_{X}(\alpha_{\tau(i)})-(m-1)\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{+}
we must have
j=\tau(i)
,
X(i)\neq\emptyset
,
m=1
; since
w_{X}(\alpha_{\tau(i)})-\alpha_{j}\in Q^{+}_{X}
it follows that
[\theta(f_{i}),f_{j}]\in\mathfrak{n}_{X}^{+}
.
The expression for
L_{1}
follows from (
A.14
);
L_{m}=0
with
m>1
is a consequence of (
3.2
).

Now suppose
w_{X}(\alpha_{\tau(i)})-(m-1)\,\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}\in\Phi^{-}\cup\{0\}
.
It follows that
X(i)=\emptyset
, so
\alpha_{i}(\rho^{\vee}_{X})\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\tau(i)\in\{i,j\}
.
If
\tau(i)=j
then
\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\cap\Phi=\{\pm\alpha_{i}\}
implies that
m\in\{1,2\}
.
Furthermore,
\theta(f_{i})=-e_{j}
and
a_{ij}=a_{ji}
.
From a successive application of (
A.14
) we obtain
L_{1}=\gamma_{j}h_{i}-\gamma_{i}h_{j}
,
L_{2}=2\left((\gamma_{j}-a_{ij}\gamma_{i})f_{i}-\gamma_{i}(\gamma_{i}-a_{ij}\gamma_{j})e_{j}\right)
and
L_{m}=0
if
m>2
, as required.

It remains to deal with the case
X(i)=\emptyset
and
\tau(i)=i
, in which case
\theta(f_{i})=-e_{i}
.
A straightforward computation gives

[\theta(f_{i}),{\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-1}(f_{j})]=(m-1)(m-2+a_{ij}){\rm ad}(f_{i})^{m-2}(f_{j}).

By virtue of the induction hypothesis, (
A.14
) simplifies to

L_{m}=(m-1)(m-1-M_{ij})\gamma_{i}\left({\rm ad}(b_{i})^{m-2}(b_{j})-L_{m-2}\right)+[b_{i},L_{m-1}],

from which the desired expression follows straightforwardly.
∎
